 Looking at the QEMU ICS register details—it's a 2-byte register with specific write masks where VALID is write-1-to-clear. The command sequence involves writing the ICW register first with the verb, then writing
 to ICS to set the busy flag while clearing the VALID bit by writing 0x02 according to the standard driver approach.
 After that, I need to poll the ICS register until the BUSY flag clears and VALID gets set, then read the response from the IRR register. Since QEMU's set_ics handler executes the command immediately, the
 response comes back synchronously rather than asynchronously.

 The GCTL register controls the reset state—clearing the CRST bit enters reset mode while setting it brings the device out of reset. The standard initialization sequence involves toggling reset, polling for the
 state change, then waiting for codec enumeration before reading the status register. In QEMU, this is handled by the reset function which clears the control register and updates the codec status bitmap
 accordingly.

 So the reset sequence involves setting GCTL to 0, triggering a device cold reset, then setting the state_sts bitmap before writing GCTL back to 1—reading STATESTS afterward gives us the codec address bitmap. I

 I need to tackle three main things: implementing BSD Socket API syscalls backed by the e1000 driver with a minimal TCP/IP stack, enabling BusyBox network utilities like ping and wget, and documenting it all in a
 README. This is substantial work, so I should start by examining the existing codebase to see what's already in place before planning out the implementation.

 I'm thinking through the scope here—the user wants a BSD Socket API implementation, BusyBox network integration, and documentation. That's substantial work, so I should map out a plan and get alignment on the
 approach. The core design will be minimal: just the essential TCP/IP layers like ARP, IPv4, ICMP, UDP, and TCP, with static arrays for simplicity rather than dynamic allocation.
 For TCP specifically, I'm aiming for something minimal but functional—a proper three-way handshake, in-order receive handling (dropping out-of-order packets), a single retransmit timer, and FIN handling. That
 should be enough to handle wget and netcat against QEMU's user-net emulation.
 I'm organizing the code into separate modules: the core network stack in one place, UDP and TCP in their own files, and the BSD socket API layer integrated with the file descriptor system. Since user programs
 will link against musl, they'll get the standard socket headers from there, so I don't need to duplicate those—though the nettest assertion program will be a musl binary that uses our socket implementation.

  ● Search(pattern: "intel_hda_set_g_ctl", path: "../../../tmp/qemu-intel-hda.c") 
  ⎿ Found 20 lines (ctrl+o to expand) 

  The GCTL register controls the reset state—clearing the CRST bit enters reset mode while setting it brings 
  the device out of reset. The standard initialization sequence involves toggling reset, polling for the 
  state change, then waiting for codec enumeration before reading the status register. In QEMU, this is 
  handled by the reset function which clears the control register and updates the codec status bitmap 
  accordingly. 

  ● Search(pattern: "state_sts", path: "../../../tmp/qemu-intel-hda.c") 
  ⎿ Found 40 lines (ctrl+o to expand) 

  So the reset sequence involves setting GCTL to 0, triggering a device cold reset, then setting the 
  state_sts bitmap before writing GCTL back to 1—reading STATESTS afterward gives us the codec address 
  bitmap. I need to be careful not to write to STATESTS since it's write-1-to-clear. Now I'm starting to 
  write the hda.c implementation, beginning with the buffer plan. 
  For the DL buffer, I'm allocating one 4096-byte page with 128-byte alignment, using 16-byte entries. Two 
  4096-byte buffers create a cyclic buffer of 8192 bytes total with CBL=8192 and LVI=1. At 48kHz, 16-bit 
  stereo that's 4 bytes per frame, so 8192 bytes gives about 2048 frames or roughly 43 milliseconds. For the 
  self-test I need LPIB to advance as the stream loops, so I'll wait 50-100ms and verify LPIB has moved. 
  QEMU's hda implementation parses the BDL and transfers data with LPIB advancing as the audio backend 
  consumes it—with the default timer setup, I should observe movement. Let me check the BDL entry format 
  requirements and then build and test to see what actually happens. 

  ● Search(pattern: "intel_hda_parse_bdl|HDA_TIMER_TICKS|B_…se_timer", path: "../../../tmp/qemu-intel-hda.c") 
  ⎿ Found 40 lines (ctrl+o to expand) 

  I'm noticing a critical ordering issue with the stream control setup — the codec's running state only gets 
  set if the stream ID matches, so SET_CHANNEL_STREAMID needs to happen before the stream actually starts, 
  otherwise it won't properly check the running state. 
  Now I'm writing the hda.c file with the register definitions and stream descriptor layout. 
  Writing register defines... Implementing the codec command walk... Finishing the codec command to check 
  pin capabilities, then storing the DAC and pin IDs if we find an output-capable pin. I'm reconsidering the 
  approach though—it'd be better to verify the pin's connection list actually contains the DAC rather than

  The byte access at offset SD_CTL is working correctly—the register lookup finds the right entry and the 
  write mask ensures only the low 8 bits get written. Now I'm moving on to implement hda.c, starting with 
  DMA memory allocation using pmm_alloc_zeroed for page-aligned physical memory and pmm_virt to get the 
  virtual pointer. I need to be careful about one thing: QEMU's pci_dma_read operates on guest physical 
  addresses, so the BDL addresses I'm working with need to be physical. 
  For the self-test, I'm setting up two buffers filled with a 440Hz square wave, creating a BDL with both 
  buffers, setting the CBL and LVI registers, then polling LPIB to watch it advance. I'm writing a polling 
  loop that checks LPIB every millisecond for up to 200ms, using timer_delay_ms to give real time to elapse 
  since QEMU's HDA timer runs on the virtual clock. Once LPIB moves, I'll stop the stream by clearing RUN 
  and report how many bytes advanced. After that I need to figure out what the total tone length should 
  represent. 
  With two 4KB buffers cycling, I get about 85ms of audio, which is enough to keep it playing for around 
  200ms before stopping. Now I need to handle the GCAP-based output stream index, update the hda_init 
  printing, and write the actual code. I also need to map HDA's BAR0 using pci_map_bar since it's a 32-bit 
  MMIO BAR on QEMU's intel-hda device.

  I'm thinking through the scope here—the user wants a BSD Socket API implementation, BusyBox network 
  integration, and documentation. That's substantial work, so I should map out a plan and get alignment on 
  the approach. The core design will be minimal: just the essential TCP/IP layers like ARP, IPv4, ICMP, UDP, 
  and TCP, with static arrays for simplicity rather than dynamic allocation. 
  For TCP specifically, I'm aiming for something minimal but functional—a proper three-way handshake, 
  in-order receive handling (dropping out-of-order packets), a single retransmit timer, and FIN handling. 
  That should be enough to handle wget and netcat against QEMU's user-net emulation. 
  I'm organizing the code into separate modules: the core network stack in one place, UDP and TCP in their 
  own files, and the BSD socket API layer integrated with the file descriptor system. Since user programs 
  will link against musl, they'll get the standard socket headers from there, so I don't need to duplicate 
  those—though the nettest assertion program will be a musl binary that uses our socket implementation.

  register semantics—I'm setting it to NRX-1 = 15 with RDH at 0, which might be preventing the receive path 
  from actually processing the looped-back packet. Checking the rx_enabled flag along with link status and 
  PCI master capability to determine if the device can receive packets. Then moving into the main receive

  `qemu_receive_packet` checks if the device can receive via `qemu_can_receive_packet`, which calls the

  packet would remain stuck in the queue indefinitely.

  ● Bash(grep -n -B4 -A12 'flush_queue_timer' /tmp/qemu-e1000.c) 
  ⎿ 123-    bool mit_timer_on;         /* Mitigation timer is running. */

  ...+138 line (ctrl+o to expand) 

  I found the issue—the `set_rx_control()` function arms a flush-queue timer for 1000 milliseconds of 
  virtual time whenever the RCTL register is written, and `e1000_can_receive()` returns false while that 
  timer is pending. 
  This means the device silently drops any packets arriving during that grace period (our loopback test and 
  the ARP reply both get queued), then delivers them once the timer fires a second later—which matches 
  exactly what we observed in the traces. The grace period is intentional QEMU behavior to prevent guests 
  from being flooded before they've set up the RX ring. 
  The fix is straightforward: after configuring RCTL, we need to wait for the flush-queue grace period to 
  expire before attempting to receive packets. QEMU_CLOCK_VIRTUAL tracks guest execution time, so real-time 
  waiting should work fine if icount is disabled. I need to check whether the PIT timer and interrupt 
  handling are available at e1000_init time, and whether I can use the PIT tick counter for a more reliable 
  sleep duration than a spin loop. 

  ● 找到了。 QEMU 的 set_rx_control()（RCTL 写处理）里有一行： 

  timer_mod(s->flush_queue_timer, qemu_clock_get_ms(QEMU_CLOCK_VIRTUAL) + 1000); 

  只要驱动写过 RCTL，之后整整 1 秒虚拟时间内 e1000_can_receive() 恒为假、e1000_receive_iov() 直接 return 
  0——网卡拒收一切。我们的自测在写完 RCTL 后几微秒就开始收，所以环回帧被静默丢弃；那条迟到的 ETH proto: 0x806

  ● Edit(src/kernel/timer.c) 
  ⎿ src/kernel/timer.c with 12 additions 

  1    #define PIT_CH0    0x40 
  2 +  #define PIT_CH2    0x42 
  3    #define PIT_CMD    0x43 
  4    #define PIT_BASE   1193182u          /* the ancient 1.193182 MHz crystal */ 
  5 
  6 +  /* Port 0x61: bit 0 is channel 2's gate input, bit 1 enables the speaker 
  7 +   * amplifier, and bit 5 reads channel 2's output pin back. */ 
  8 +  #define PORT_61    0x61 
  9 + 
  10    static volatile uint64_t g_ticks; 
  11 
  12    static inline void outb(uint16_t port, uint8_t v) 
  13    { 
  14        asm volatile("outb %0, %1" :: "a"(v), "Nd"(port)); 
  15    } 
  16 + 
  17 +  static inline uint8_t inb(uint16_t port) 
  18 +  { 
  19 +      uint8_t v; 
  20 +      asm volatile("inb %1, %0" : "=a"(v) : "Nd"(port)); 
  21 +      return v; 
  22 +  } 

  ● Edit(src/kernel/timer.c) 
  ⎿ src/kernel/timer.c with 37 additions 

  2    { 
  3        return g_ticks; 
  4    } 
  5 + 
  6 +  /* 
  7 +   * A delay measured in real time instead of in loop iterations. 
  8 +   * 
  9 +   * Channel 0 belongs to the scheduler, so this borrows channel 2 -- the one 
  10 +   * wired to the PC speaker -- as a one-shot stopwatch: load a count, raise the 
  11 +   * gate, and poll until the output pin goes high at terminal count.  Bit 1 of 
  12 +   * port 0x61, the speaker enable, is left clear throughout, so nothing beeps. 
  13 +   * 
  14 +   * Two properties matter to callers.  It needs no interrupts, so it is usable 
  15 +   * during early boot while IRQ 0 is still masked; and a millisecond here is an 
  16 +   * actual millisecond, which a loop of io_delay() can never promise.  Device 
  17 +   * bring-up is full of "wait N ms after this write" requirements, and guessing 
  18 +   * at them with spin counts is how drivers become machine-specific. 
  19 +   */ 
  20 +  void timer_delay_ms(unsigned ms) 
  21 +  { 
  22 +      const uint16_t count = (uint16_t)(PIT_BASE / 1000u);   /* one millisecond */ 
  23 + 
  24 +      while (ms--) { 
  25 +          uint8_t p61 = (uint8_t)(inb(PORT_61) & ~0x02u);    /* speaker muted */ 
  26 + 
  27 +          outb(PORT_61, (uint8_t)(p61 & ~0x01u));   /* gate low: counter held */ 
  28 +          outb(PIT_CMD, 0xB0);                      /* ch2, lo/hi, mode 0 */ 
  29 +          outb(PIT_CH2, (uint8_t)(count & 0xFF)); 
  30 +          outb(PIT_CH2, (uint8_t)(count >> 8)); 
  31 +          outb(PORT_61, (uint8_t)(p61 | 0x01u));    /* gate high: counting */ 
  32 + 
  33 +          /* Bounded: on a board that does not route channel 2 back to port 
  34 +           * 0x61 this must cost a moment, not the boot. */ 
  35 +          uint32_t i = 0; 
  36 +          while (!(inb(PORT_61) & 0x20)) { 
  37 +              if (++i == 200000u) 
  38 +                  return; 
  39 +          } 
  40 +      } 
  41 +  }

  ⎿ src/kernel/e1000.c with 24 additions 

  1 +  /* 
  2 +   * Both self-tests need the receiver to actually work, and under QEMU it does 
  3 +   * not work for the first second after RCTL is written. 
  4 +   * 
  5 +   * QEMU arms a one-second "flush queue" timer on every write to RCTL, and 
  6 +   * while that timer is pending e1000_can_receive() reports false and 
  7 +   * e1000_receive_iov() returns immediately -- every inbound frame is dropped 
  8 +   * without a trace, without a statistic, and without touching the ring.  The 
  9 +   * intent is to give a guest that enables the receiver before it has finished 
  10 +   * building descriptors a moment to catch up.  The effect on a driver that 
  11 +   * builds the ring *first* and then tests it is a receiver that is silently 
  12 +   * dead exactly when the test runs. 
  13 +   * 
  14 +   * Real 82540EMs have no such window, so this wait is emulator etiquette 
  15 +   * rather than hardware programming -- but it costs one second once, and 
  16 +   * without it the tests below report a nonexistent bug. 
  17 +   */ 
  18 +  static void wait_out_rx_grace_period(void) 
  19 +  { 
  20 +      timer_delay_ms(1100); 
  21 +  } 
  22 + 
  23    int e1000_selftest(void)

  The immediate command interface requires writing the verb to the ICW register at 0x60, setting the BUSY 
  bit in ICS, and then reading the response from IRR at 0x64 once the BUSY bit clears and the VALID bit 
  sets. Since the ICS write mask is 0x0003 with a clear mask of 0x0002, writing 1 to bit1 actually clears 
  it, so I need to write 0x0001 to set BUSY while clearing VALID. 
  The standard sequence is: clear VALID first by writing 0x0002 to ICS, write the verb to ICW, then write 
  0x0001 to ICS to trigger the command synchronously, poll until BUSY clears and VALID sets, and finally 
  read the response from IRR. The whandler checks the BUSY bit and invokes the command execution path 
  through the corb run logic. 
  Now I'm looking at the stream descriptor register offsets in QEMU to understand the layout of control, 
  status, position, and buffer configuration registers.

  Looking at how QEMU's HDA implementation handles MMIO register access, I need to understand the size 
  constraints—the register table enforces specific widths for reads and writes, and mismatched sizes might 
  get rejected. Let me trace through the MMIO write handler to see how it validates these operations. 
  The write handler looks up the register in the table and applies a write mask based on the access width, 
  then only allows bits that are both in the access width and marked as writable in the register definition. 
  Register offsets are fixed—for example, SDnCTL occupies exactly 4 bytes at its designated offset. 
  When reading or writing multi-byte values, the mask gets applied to ensure only the appropriate bits are 
  modified. I'm planning out the specific register accesses needed: GCAP as a 16-bit read at offset 0x00,

  Looking at the QEMU ICS register details—it's a 2-byte register with specific write masks where VALID is 
  write-1-to-clear. The command sequence involves writing the ICW register first with the verb, then writing

  approach. 
  After that, I need to poll the ICS register until the BUSY flag clears and VALID gets set, then read the

  The GCTL register controls the reset state—clearing the CRST bit enters reset mode while setting it brings 
  the device out of reset. The standard initialization sequence involves toggling reset, polling for the 
  state change, then waiting for codec enumeration before reading the status register. In QEMU, this is 
  handled by the reset function which clears the control register and updates the codec status bitmap 
  accordingly.

  ● Search(pattern: "intel_hda_set_g_ctl", path: "../../../tmp/qemu-intel-hda.c") 
  ⎿ Found 20 lines (ctrl+o to expand) 

  The GCTL register controls the reset state—clearing the CRST bit enters reset mode while setting it 
  brings 
  the device out of reset. The standard initialization sequence involves toggling reset, polling for the 
  state change, then waiting for codec enumeration before reading the status register. In QEMU, this is 
  handled by the reset function which clears the control register and updates the codec status bitmap 
  accordingly. 

  ● Search(pattern: "state_sts", path: "../../../tmp/qemu-intel-hda.c") 
  ⎿ Found 40 lines (ctrl+o to expand) 

  So the reset sequence involves setting GCTL to 0, triggering a device cold reset, then setting the 
  state_sts bitmap before writing GCTL back to 1—reading STATESTS afterward gives us the codec address 
  bitmap. I need to be careful not to write to STATESTS since it's write-1-to-clear. Now I'm starting to 
  write the hda.c implementation, beginning with the buffer plan. 
  For the DL buffer, I'm allocating one 4096-byte page with 128-byte alignment, using 16-byte entries. 
  Two 
  4096-byte buffers create a cyclic buffer of 8192 bytes total with CBL=8192 and LVI=1. At 48kHz, 16-bit 
  stereo that's 4 bytes per frame, so 8192 bytes gives about 2048 frames or roughly 43 milliseconds. For 
  the 
  self-test I need LPIB to advance as the stream loops, so I'll wait 50-100ms and verify LPIB has moved. 
  QEMU's hda implementation parses the BDL and transfers data with LPIB advancing as the audio backend 
  consumes it—with the default timer setup, I should observe movement. Let me check the BDL entry format 
  requirements and then build and test to see what actually happens.

● src/kernel/tcp.c 写完（~560 行），用内核编译参数 -Wall -Wextra -O2 单独编译零告警。

  实现要点

  - PCB 池：g_pcbs[8] 静态数组，每条连接收发各占一个 pmm_alloc_zeroed() 页（4 KiB 环）。
  - 序号算术：全部经 seq_lt/seq_leq 有符号差比较，32 位回绕无感。
  - 环即序号空间：slot = seq & 4095，收环持 [rcv_read, rcv_nxt)，发环持 [snd_una, snd_end)，无需其他簿记。
  - 状态机全量：三次握手（SYN_SENT/SYN_RCVD）、按序接收（乱序丢 + dup ACK）、累积 ACK、FIN 双半关闭（FIN_WAIT_1/2、CLOSING、CLOSE_WAIT、LAST_ACK）、TIME_WAIT 2s、LISTEN 的 4 槽 accept 队列（溢出
  RST，与真栈一致）。
  - 定时器：单一固定 RTO=1s、5 次重传上限 → E_TIMEDOUT；connect 5s 超时；全在 tcp_tick()（100 Hz）。
  - 发送：tcp_output() 按「对端窗口 − 在途」发新数据，FIN 在数据排空后附出；重传从 snd_una 起，FIN 自动重附。
  - RST 语义：tcp_destroy() 对仍打开的连接发 RST（正是 close 未读 socket 的行为）；无匹配 PCB 的段按 RFC 793 回 RST；入向 RST 在 SYN_SENT 映射 E_CONNREFUSED、其余 E_CONNRESET，PCB 留存供 SO_ERROR 取走。
  - 选项：只解析/只发 MSS；SYN 带 MSS=1460。
  - 16 KiB 内核栈下 tcp_emit 的 1.5 KB 栈缓冲安全。

  写的过程中修掉了自己留下的两处瑕疵（一个空调试 if、一个 statement-expression 三元），并给 net.c 补了 #include "tcp.h"。剩余的唯一隐式声明是 sock_init()，属 Task #29 的 sock.h。

  下一步按 TaskList 是 #29 sock.{c,h}（socket 对象 + UDP + RAW），写完即可整体构建。

● Task #29 完成。src/kernel/sock.h（~95 行）+ src/kernel/sock.c（~640 行）。

  sock.h — 层间契约
  - AF_INET / SOCK_STREAM|DGRAM|RAW / IPPROTO_* / SOL_SOCKET / SO_* / SHUT_* / MSG_PEEK，全用 Linux 号（musl 原样透传）。
  - 索引而非指针跨界（sock_create 返回 index，stale fd 不可能指到回收对象）。
  - 地址一律主机序；sockaddr 解析留给 syscall 层（#30）。

  sock.c — 三块核心

  1. 数据报环（UDP/RAW 共用）：每 socket 一个 pmm 页，记录 = 8 字节头 {src_ip, src_port, len} + 8 字节对齐
  payload。对齐不是为速度——它保证「环绕标记（len=0xFFFF）永远放得下」，读写双方对环绕位置永不分歧。记录不跨界，payload 拷贝因此不需要处理回绕。
  2. 阻塞范式：SOCK_WAIT_LOOP 宏（C 没有闭包），完全抄管道的 cli→查→sched_block_irqoff(WAIT_NET)→重查，外加每轮 net_poll()——环回上唤醒我们的包已经在队列里，只有 poll 能把它变成我们在等的状态变化。支持
  nonblock（-E_AGAIN）与信号中断（-E_INTR）。proc.h 加了 WAIT_NET。
  3. 三种类型分派：
    - UDP：bind 冲突检查、ephemeral 端口、首发包自动绑端口、校验和（0 结果改写 0xFFFF）、广播需 SO_BROADCAST 否则 -E_PERM、无监听静默丢（注释说明为何不造 ICMP port unreachable）。
    - RAW：raw_input 按 protocol 过滤分发从 IP 头开始的整包（BusyBox ping 的解析前提）；发送直接 net_ip_output。
    - STREAM：connect 手写等待循环（区分 ESTABLISHED/CLOSED 并取走 pending error，非阻塞重poll 得 -E_ALREADY）；accept 从监听队列取子 PCB 换新 socket 槽；send 窗口满时阻塞，连接断开时 proc_signal(SIGPIPE) +
  -E_PIPE；recv 区分「暂无数据 / EOF / 带错关闭」；shutdown(SHUT_WR) → tcp_close_write。
  - setsockopt 宽容策略：未知 option 返回 0（ping 的初始化路径依赖这个），SO_BROADCAST 真存，SO_ERROR 读取即消费。
  - 附带修了 tcp.c 一个悬挂指针：listener 提前关闭时子 PCB 握手完成会写已回收的 accept 队列，加 par->used 守卫。

  至此 net/tcp/sock 三层自洽，全栈编译干净。下一步 #30 接线：vfs 加 VFS_SOCKET、sysnum.h 加 41–55、syscall dispatch、e1000_irq→net_poll+唤醒、timer→net_tick。

 "可在 syscall 上下文用、不能在中断里用" per my earlier analysis (it busy-spins up to 100k io_delay). Hmm. But wait — tcp retransmit from tcp_tick would call e1000_send from interrupt context. Is that a problem?
 e1000_send spins on TXD DD bit — QEMU sets it immediately. The "cannot in interrupt" concern was about long spins; in practice with QEMU it's fine, on real hw could spin 100k io_delays in IRQ = bad but tolerable
